From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well... my wife made the appointment. She's the one who's scared. We've been in that house for fifteen years, and honestly, the maintenance is just... it's a lot. I haven't been working, so I try to keep an eye on things, but the plumbing is old. We had a guy out a few years ago, charged us an arm and a leg just to look at the water heater. He drove this loud yellow van that leaked oil all over my driveway, which took me two days to scrub out. \n\nBut anyway, what finally scared her—why she made me come in—is that I keep hearing water running inside the walls. And men whispering. Just... whispering in the walls, when nobody’s there. I thought maybe it was the neighbors at first, but our houses aren't even that close. And sometimes I see this dark, wet patch spreading across the hallway floor. I go over, I touch it, I check it... and it's dry. Completely dry. I used to lay down towels, you know, the good blue ones we got for our anniversary, just in case the wood warped, but... I don't know. My brother had water damage once and it completely ruined his foundation, so you can't be too careful.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been hearing the whispering in the walls?\n\nPatient: Well, let's see... it's been going on for about seven months now. Yeah, right around the time of that awful winter freeze we had. You remember how bad the roads got? I actually had to go out in it because my wife's sedan has terrible traction. Those tires were practically bald since we bought them at that discount place off the interstate. I told her not to go there, the guys in the shop always try to upsell you on alignment packages you don't even need, but she likes a bargain. \n\nAnyway, the freeze... it just turned all the house repairs I was trying to manage into this massive mess. I just couldn't get out from under it. I had been trying to patch up the baseboards in the guest room, and then the cold hit, and the materials wouldn't set right, and I was spending hours just sitting on the floor staring at the putty... I don't know. It just felt like everything was piling up and collapsing all at once. But yeah, that's when I started hearing them. Seven months. Ever since the freeze ruined everything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the whispering in the walls is actually people talking, or could it be something else?\n\nPatient: People talking? Well... I know it sounds far-fetched. I really do. But I’m certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. Everything. \n\nThe wiring is that old copper stuff, you know? We had an electrician out back in... oh, it must have been 2008. He had this little terrier that just sat in the front seat of his truck the whole time, barking its head off while he looked at our breaker box. But he didn't even fix the moisture problem. He just charged us and left. So now the dampness and the bad wires, it's all mixed together and it's just... toxic. It seeped into my clothes, my old work boots, my papers... everything is ruined. \n\nAnd the whispering... well, I can tell the neighbors are watching to see when I mess up again. Gary across the street is always out there messing with his sprinkler heads. Nobody needs to adjust their sprinklers three times a week. He just stands there on his lawn, pretending to work on them, but he's really just staring at my windows, waiting for me to finally collapse.\n\nDoctor: Do you really think Gary is watching you, or is that just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: It's not a feeling. I know how it sounds to you, I know it sounds far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain he's watching me. They all are. They're just waiting to see when I mess up again. And they know about the house. I'm telling you, the mold and that old wiring, it has completely poisoned everything I own. Everything in there is contaminated. My clothes, my tools... \n\nI had this really good set of Craftsman wrenches I bought at Sears, back when the one at the mall was still open. You remember that mall? They tore the whole thing down a few years ago to build those luxury apartments. The ones with those weird glass balconies. I don't know who would want to sit on a glass balcony right over the interstate, the noise must be terrible. My wife's sister lives near a highway and she can't even open her windows in the summer because of the exhaust from the semi-trucks. She tried planting these tall arborvitae bushes to block the sound, but they all died because the soil was too acidic. \n\nBut... what was I saying? Oh, the wrenches. They're ruined. Poisoned by the house. And Gary knows it. He just stands out there pretending to adjust his sprinklers, just watching my front door, waiting for me to completely fall apart.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can do anything to stop Gary from watching you or to prevent the house from getting worse?\n\nPatient: Stop him? No... I don't think I can. I tried pulling the blinds down, but they're those cheap plastic ones, you know? We got them at Home Depot back when they had that huge clearance sale. Actually, it was the exact same day I bought this heavy-duty bleach to try and scrub the walls. People think bleach kills mold, but it really doesn't. It just bleaches the spores white so you can't see them. My buddy Dave told me that. He used to do HVAC work before he ruined his back. He slipped on some black ice outside a gas station and broke two vertebrae. He had to wear one of those awful halo braces for months. It looked so uncomfortable, having metal literally screwed into your skull... I don't know how he slept. He said he had to sleep sitting straight up in a brown leather recliner for half a year. \n\nBut... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, the house. Right. Like I said before, I know it sounds far-fetched. I know exactly how it sounds to a doctor. But I am just... I'm certain the mold and that wiring have poisoned everything I own. I can't stop it from getting worse because it's already in everything, the damage is done. And I can't stop Gary. I can just tell he and the rest of the neighbors are watching, just waiting for me to finally mess up for good. There's just nothing I can do about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any control over what happens to you and your house?\n\nPatient: Control? No, no control at all. I mean, how can I? Everything is already ruined. I haven't been able to keep up with any of it... the repairs, paying the bills, none of it. My wife, she’s had to take over everything. All the decisions I used to handle, she’s doing it now. She's been so stressed, I can see it in her face. She used to love gardening to relax, you know? She had these beautiful tomato plants out back, these huge Big Boys and some cherry tomatoes. We used to make our own salsa every August. We'd get the jalapeños from the farmer's market downtown... the one they hold on Saturdays next to the old courthouse. They have this guy there who sells the most incredible raw honey. He keeps his bees out by the county line, near where that old drive-in theater used to be before it burned down in the nineties. I remember going there when I was a kid to see a double feature, sitting in the back of my dad's station wagon... \n\nBut, right, the house. Yeah, I can't do anything about it. Things got so bad that my wife actually called one of those crisis teams on me. She had to. There was some kind of safety inspection they wanted to do at the house, and I just couldn't cooperate. I couldn't let them in. Because if they came in, they'd see the poison, or they'd track more of it around, and Gary would see them, and... I just couldn't do it. So, no. I don't have any control left.\n\nDoctor: Do you think Gary or the neighbors are plotting against you specifically?\n\nPatient: Plotting? I don't know if they have, like, secret meetings about it, but yes, it's me they're focused on. Like I keep saying... and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you, I really do... but I am absolutely certain they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. \n\nGary especially. He'll stand out by his mailbox forever, just staring at my place. He gets his mail from that same guy we've had on our route for fifteen years. Carl, I think his name is. Carl always wears those blue uniform shorts, even in the dead of December. I don't understand how his legs don't freeze. I bought a pair of heavy thermal long johns once for a hunting trip, back when I used to hunt with my brother-in-law. We went up to this little cabin near the Canadian border, and it was so cold the pipes froze solid the very first night. We had to scoop snow into a pot and melt it on the woodstove just to make our morning coffee. The coffee tasted awful, like pine needles and ash, but we drank it anyway because we were just shivering so hard... \n\nWait, why was I talking about the cabin? Oh, Gary. Right. No, he's watching me. They all are. And they know my house is ruined. I know I've said it a hundred times today, but the mold and that bad wiring... it has poisoned everything I own. Everything is just contaminated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think Gary or the neighbors want to harm you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Like... physically? No, I don't think they're going to come over here and, you know, attack me or anything. It's not like that. It's more like they're just waiting for me to fail. Like I told you, I know how crazy it sounds, I really do. But I'm telling you, I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Everything is ruined. And I can just tell the neighbors are watching, waiting to see when I mess up again. \n\nIt's like they have nothing better to do with their time. Especially since the neighborhood association stopped doing those monthly block parties. They used to have them every first Sunday of the month, right down at the end of the cul-de-sac. Someone would always bring this massive inflatable bounce house for the kids. One time, it got really windy, and the stakes came loose, and the whole thing almost blew into Mrs. Gable's prized rose bushes. She would have been furious. She spends hours out there with these tiny little pruning shears, clipping away at them. She won first prize at the county fair three years in a row for her yellow roses. My wife tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. We tried spraying them with this organic neem oil stuff, but it smelled terrible, like rancid garlic and peanut butter...\n\nBut, yeah, no, they don't want to hurt me physically. They're just watching. Waiting for me to finally crack.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors are talking about you specifically when they're outside?\n\nPatient: Talking about me? Yeah... I mean, I see them out there, leaning over the property lines. They whisper. It’s like high school all over again. My daughter hated high school for that exact reason. She went to West Monroe, and the girls there were just brutal. She had this one friend, Sarah, who spread a rumor about her stealing a graphing calculator from the math lab. Can you believe that? A TI-84. Those things were like a hundred bucks back then. I remember buying it at Circuit City before they all closed down. Circuit City had that big red plug logo... I used to love wandering the aisles there on weekends, looking at the huge stereo systems. We bought our first CD player there in the nineties, it was a Sony, held five discs at once... \n\nBut, um, what were we... right, the neighbors. Yes, they talk about me. I know I keep saying this today, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but they are definitely watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. They know about the house. They know that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. They're just standing out there whispering about it, waiting for me to finally lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' conversations are specifically about you when they whisper?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when they whisper... I mean, what else would they be whispering about? It's always when I'm out there or when they think I'm looking out the window. They huddle up. Just like those guys at the hardware store used to do. I worked at a True Value for about six months back in my twenties. The manager, this guy named Phil, he was always whispering with the cashiers. Phil drove this beat-up old El Camino, faded blue with a rusted-out tailgate. He was so proud of that car, always talking about how he was going to restore it, put a new engine in it, paint it cherry red. He kept a bucket of those pine tree air fresheners in the cab, so it always smelled like cheap cologne and cedar... \n\nAnyway, I don't even know why I thought of Phil. But yes, the neighbors. They're whispering about me. Like I keep telling you—and I know it sounds completely far-fetched, I really do—but I am certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting to see when I mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' whispering is directly about you or something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's about me. I mean, they're not whispering about the weather, you know? They look right at my house. It's like when you go to a restaurant and you know the waiter is talking about you to the bartender. We used to go to this diner, Mel's, over on 5th Street. They had the best cherry pie. Not that canned stuff, but real cherries, tart and sweet. My wife loved it. We'd go every Friday night, sit in the same red vinyl booth... booth number four, right by the jukebox. That jukebox had all the classics. I remember playing 'Dock of the Bay' on it once, and the needle got stuck, just kept repeating the same whistled note over and over until the cook had to come out and smack the side of the machine. He had this big grease stain on his apron...\n\nBut, uh, anyway, I know I've said this a hundred times today, and I know how crazy it sounds. I really do. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they know it. They're just watching, whispering, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control what you think about, or do your thoughts feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control my thoughts? I don't know... I guess I try to, but they just sort of go where they want. It's like trying to steer a car when the alignment is shot. I had this old Ford Taurus back in the late nineties, and the steering on that thing was just awful. If you took your hands off the wheel for even a second, it would pull hard to the right. I remember driving it down I-95 in the pouring rain, the wipers were making this terrible screeching noise because the rubber was completely worn down. I meant to replace them at AutoZone, but I kept forgetting. AutoZone always smelled like fresh tires and motor oil... I used to buy my windshield wiper fluid there, the kind that melts the ice in the winter. Winter used to be so much colder when I was a kid...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? My thoughts... right. I don't know if they control me, exactly. But I can't stop thinking about the house. I know I keep bringing it up, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. I can't stop thinking about it because it's true, and I know the neighbors are just waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, or do they seem disjointed and hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Connected? I mean, they make sense to me, mostly. It's just... there's a lot of them. Like a string of lights where one bulb goes out, and you have to check every single one to find the bad one. We had these old Christmas lights when I was a kid, the big glass ones, C9s I think they called them. They got so hot you could actually burn your fingers on them. My dad used to string them up around the gutters every Thanksgiving weekend. He had this aluminum ladder that was always wobbly on the left side because the rubber foot fell off. He tried fixing it with duct tape, but duct tape never really holds up in the cold, does it? It just gets brittle and peels right off. I used to hate holding the bottom of that ladder, my hands would just freeze... \n\nBut, uh, disjointed... I don't know. Maybe they are. It's hard to tell. But the main thing, the thing that never leaves my head—and I know I keep saying this, I know exactly how crazy it sounds—but I am absolutely positive that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated and ruined. And those neighbors, they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting for me to make another mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one thought?\n\nPatient: Focused? Yeah, I guess it's hard. It's like... it's like trying to watch television when the signal keeps dropping out. You know, back before cable, when you had to mess with the rabbit ears? My granddad had this old Zenith console TV. The thing was a piece of furniture, solid wood, weighed a ton. We used to have to wrap aluminum foil around the tips of the antennas just to get channel 3 to come in clear so we could watch the evening news. He always watched Walter Cronkite. Said he was the only man on television you could trust. He had this big recliner, olive green corduroy, and it squeaked every time he leaned back. I tried oiling the springs once with WD-40, but it just made the whole living room smell like a garage... \n\nBut, um, staying focused... I don't really know. I guess I lose track of things sometimes. But the one thing I can't lose track of—and I know you're probably sick of hearing it, I know how it sounds—but I am absolutely sure that the mold and the bad wiring in my house have poisoned every single thing I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they're just waiting, watching me, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you're worried about them, or because you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Worried about them? I mean, yeah, I'm worried, but it's more like... it's just always there. Like a hum in the background that you can't turn off. Reminds me of this old refrigerator I had in my first apartment. It was this ugly avocado green color, from the seventies, and the compressor was going bad. It made this constant, low buzzing sound, day and night. At first, it drove me absolutely up the wall, but eventually, I sort of got used to it. Until it finally died right before a long weekend, and all my groceries went bad. I had just bought a whole gallon of milk, too. Milk used to come in those thick glass bottles when I was really little, and the cream would rise to the top... I remember the milkman leaving them on the porch in this little wire carrier. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop thinking about it. Yeah, I can't stop. Because it's not just a worry, it's... it's a fact. I know I've said it a hundred times, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Every single thing. And the neighbors, I can tell they're just watching me, waiting to see when I mess up again. They're just waiting for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you can't stop, or because you believe it's really happening?\n\nPatient: Because it's really happening? Yeah. I mean, it's not just a feeling or a worry. It's like... it's like when you know something is wrong with your car, but the mechanic tells you it's fine. I had this '98 Ford Taurus back in the day, and the transmission kept slipping. The guy at the shop—Earl, I think his name was, he had this grease stain on his cap that looked exactly like the state of Nevada—he kept telling me it was just the cold weather making it shift hard. But you can feel it, you know? When the gears are just grinding together. I ended up breaking down on the interstate right near the county line. Had to wait three hours for a tow truck in the freezing rain. The driver was eating these little powdered donuts and got white sugar powder all over my steering wheel and the floor mats. I never could get that powder completely out of the carpet... \n\nBut, um... yeah. Because it's real. I know I keep repeating myself today, and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you. You probably think I've completely lost my mind. But I am absolutely certain that it's really happening. The mold, the wiring in that house—it actually poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors, they aren't just in my head either. I can tell they're out there watching me, just waiting to see when I'm gonna mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever actually seen the neighbors doing something that makes you think they're watching you, or is it just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: Seen them? Well, it's not just a feeling, I mean... it's like when you're looking through binoculars and you just know something's moving in the trees. I used to have this really nice pair of Bushnell binoculars. Heavy ones, with the black rubber coating on the outside. My uncle gave them to me right after I got my associate's degree. We used to go out to Miller's Pond in the early mornings to look for herons. Have you ever seen a great blue heron up close? They look exactly like dinosaurs. Just these long, scrawny legs and these sharp, spear-like beaks. They stand so perfectly still in the shallow water you'd think they were statues. Anyway, I dropped those binoculars on the concrete driveway a few years back and cracked the right lens right down the middle. I tried to fix it with a little dab of superglue, but it just made everything look foggy and gave me a massive headache whenever I tried to look through them...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, seeing the neighbors. I don't need binoculars for that. Look, I know I'm a broken record today. I know I keep saying the exact same thing, and I know how completely far-fetched it sounds to you. But I am absolutely certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors... I can just tell they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. I can just tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. She spends hours out there with these tiny little pruning shears, clipping away at them. She won first prize at the county fair three years in a row for her yellow roses. We tried spraying them with this organic neem oil stuff, but it smelled terrible, like rancid garlic and peanut butter...\n\nBut, yeah, no, they don't want to hurt me physically. They're just watching. Waiting for me to finally crack.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors are talking about you specifically when they're outside?\n\nPatient: Talking about me? Yeah... I mean, I see them out there, leaning over the property lines. They whisper. It’s like high school all over again. My daughter hated high school for that exact reason. She went to West Monroe, and the girls there were just brutal. She had this one friend, Sarah, who spread a rumor about her stealing a graphing calculator from the math lab. Can you believe that? A TI-84. Those things were like a hundred bucks back then. I remember buying it at Circuit City before they all closed down. Circuit City had that big red plug logo... I used to love wandering the aisles there on weekends, looking at the huge stereo systems. We bought our first CD player there in the nineties, it was a Sony, held five discs at once... \n\nBut, um, what were we... right, the neighbors. Yes, they talk about me. I know I keep saying this today, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but they are definitely watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. They know about the house. They know that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. They're just standing out there whispering about it, waiting for me to finally lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' conversations are specifically about you when they whisper?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when they whisper... I mean, what else would they be whispering about? It's always when I'm out there or when they think I'm looking out the window. They huddle up. Just like those guys at the hardware store used to do. I worked at a True Value for about six months back in my twenties. The manager, this guy named Phil, he was always whispering with the cashiers. Phil drove this beat-up old El Camino, faded blue with a rusted-out tailgate. He was so proud of that car, always talking about how he was going to restore it, put a new engine in it, paint it cherry red. He kept a bucket of those pine tree air fresheners in the cab, so it always smelled like cheap cologne and cedar... \n\nAnyway, I don't even know why I thought of Phil. But yes, the neighbors. They're whispering about me. Like I keep telling you—and I know it sounds completely far-fetched, I really do—but I am certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting to see when I mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' whispering is directly about you or something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's about me. I mean, they're not whispering about the weather, you know? They look right at my house. It's like when you go to a restaurant and you know the waiter is talking about you to the bartender. We used to go to this diner, Mel's, over on 5th Street. They had the best cherry pie. Not that canned stuff, but real cherries, tart and sweet. My wife loved it. We'd go every Friday night, sit in the same red vinyl booth... booth number four, right by the jukebox. That jukebox had all the classics. I remember playing 'Dock of the Bay' on it once, and the needle got stuck, just kept repeating the same whistled note over and over until the cook had to come out and smack the side of the machine. He had this big grease stain on his apron...\n\nBut, uh, anyway, I know I've said this a hundred times today, and I know how crazy it sounds. I really do. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they know it. They're just watching, whispering, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control what you think about, or do your thoughts feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control my thoughts? I don't know... I guess I try to, but they just sort of go where they want. It's like trying to steer a car when the alignment is shot. I had this old Ford Taurus back in the late nineties, and the steering on that thing was just awful. If you took your hands off the wheel for even a second, it would pull hard to the right. I remember driving it down I-95 in the pouring rain, the wipers were making this terrible screeching noise because the rubber was completely worn down. I meant to replace them at AutoZone, but I kept forgetting. AutoZone always smelled like fresh tires and motor oil... I used to buy my windshield wiper fluid there, the kind that melts the ice in the winter. Winter used to be so much colder when I was a kid...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? My thoughts... right. I don't know if they control me, exactly. But I can't stop thinking about the house. I know I keep bringing it up, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. I can't stop thinking about it because it's true, and I know the neighbors are just waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, or do they seem disjointed and hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Connected? I mean, they make sense to me, mostly. It's just... there's a lot of them. Like a string of lights where one bulb goes out, and you have to check every single one to find the bad one. We had these old Christmas lights when I was a kid, the big glass ones, C9s I think they called them. They got so hot you could actually burn your fingers on them. My dad used to string them up around the gutters every Thanksgiving weekend. He had this aluminum ladder that was always wobbly on the left side because the rubber foot fell off. He tried fixing it with duct tape, but duct tape never really holds up in the cold, does it? It just gets brittle and peels right off. I used to hate holding the bottom of that ladder, my hands would just freeze... \n\nBut, uh, disjointed... I don't know. Maybe they are. It's hard to tell. But the main thing, the thing that never leaves my head—and I know I keep saying this, I know exactly how crazy it sounds—but I am absolutely positive that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated and ruined. And those neighbors, they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting for me to make another mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one thought?\n\nPatient: Focused? Yeah, I guess it's hard. It's like... it's like trying to watch television when the signal keeps dropping out. You know, back before cable, when you had to mess with the rabbit ears? My granddad had this old Zenith console TV. The thing was a piece of furniture, solid wood, weighed a ton. We used to have to wrap aluminum foil around the tips of the antennas just to get channel 3 to come in clear so we could watch the evening news. He always watched Walter Cronkite. Said he was the only man on television you could trust. He had this big recliner, olive green corduroy, and it squeaked every time he leaned back. I tried oiling the springs once with WD-40, but it just made the whole living room smell like a garage... \n\nBut, um, staying focused... I don't really know. I guess I lose track of things sometimes. But the one thing I can't lose track of—and I know you're probably sick of hearing it, I know how it sounds—but I am absolutely sure that the mold and the bad wiring in my house have poisoned every single thing I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they're just waiting, watching me, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you're worried about them, or because you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Worried about them? I mean, yeah, I'm worried, but it's more like... it's just always there. Like a hum in the background that you can't turn off. Reminds me of this old refrigerator I had in my first apartment. It was this ugly avocado green color, from the seventies, and the compressor was going bad. It made this constant, low buzzing sound, day and night. At first, it drove me absolutely up the wall, but eventually, I sort of got used to it. Until it finally died right before a long weekend, and all my groceries went bad. I had just bought a whole gallon of milk, too. Milk used to come in those thick glass bottles when I was really little, and the cream would rise to the top... I remember the milkman leaving them on the porch in this little wire carrier. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop thinking about it. Yeah, I can't stop. Because it's not just a worry, it's... it's a fact. I know I've said it a hundred times, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Every single thing. And the neighbors, I can tell they're just watching me, waiting to see when I mess up again. They're just waiting for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you can't stop, or because you believe it's really happening?\n\nPatient: Because it's really happening? Yeah. I mean, it's not just a feeling or a worry. It's like... it's like when you know something is wrong with your car, but the mechanic tells you it's fine. I had this '98 Ford Taurus back in the day, and the transmission kept slipping. The guy at the shop—Earl, I think his name was, he had this grease stain on his cap that looked exactly like the state of Nevada—he kept telling me it was just the cold weather making it shift hard. But you can feel it, you know? When the gears are just grinding together. I ended up breaking down on the interstate right near the county line. Had to wait three hours for a tow truck in the freezing rain. The driver was eating these little powdered donuts and got white sugar powder all over my steering wheel and the floor mats. I never could get that powder completely out of the carpet... \n\nBut, um... yeah. Because it's real. I know I keep repeating myself today, and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you. You probably think I've completely lost my mind. But I am absolutely certain that it's really happening. The mold, the wiring in that house—it actually poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors, they aren't just in my head either. I can tell they're out there watching me, just waiting to see when I'm gonna mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever actually seen the neighbors doing something that makes you think they're watching you, or is it just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: Seen them? Well, it's not just a feeling, I mean... it's like when you're looking through binoculars and you just know something's moving in the trees. I used to have this really nice pair of Bushnell binoculars. Heavy ones, with the black rubber coating on the outside. My uncle gave them to me right after I got my associate's degree. We used to go out to Miller's Pond in the early mornings to look for herons. Have you ever seen a great blue heron up close? They look exactly like dinosaurs. Just these long, scrawny legs and these sharp, spear-like beaks. They stand so perfectly still in the shallow water you'd think they were statues. Anyway, I dropped those binoculars on the concrete driveway a few years back and cracked the right lens right down the middle. I tried to fix it with a little dab of superglue, but it just made everything look foggy and gave me a massive headache whenever I tried to look through them...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, seeing the neighbors. I don't need binoculars for that. Look, I know I'm a broken record today. I know I keep saying the exact same thing, and I know how completely far-fetched it sounds to you. But I am absolutely certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors... I can just tell they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. I can just tell.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Moved to a different house? I... I don't know. Maybe? But moving is such a hassle. I helped my cousin move a few years ago. He had this massive, solid oak dining table. Must have weighed three hundred pounds. We had to pivot it around this tiny landing in his apartment building, and my cousin—he’s always been a little clumsy—he slipped on a piece of junk mail that was on the floor. It was one of those glossy circulars for a pizza place, the kind with the coupons you have to cut out. He went down hard and the table pinned his leg against the banister. We had to call the fire department to come get it off him. The paramedics gave him a little ice pack that you squeeze to make it cold... I always wondered what kind of chemicals are inside those things to make them freeze instantly like that. It can't be good for the environment.

But, uh, moving away... I mean, I know I've said this a dozen times already, and I know how completely crazy it sounds. You probably think I'm making it all up. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors... even if I moved, I feel like they'd still be watching me. They're just waiting for me to mess up again. I can just feel it.

It looked so uncomfortable, having metal literally screwed into your skull... I don't know how he slept. He said he had to sleep sitting straight up in a brown leather recliner for half a year. \n\nBut... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, the house. Right. Like I said before, I know it sounds far-fetched. I know exactly how it sounds to a doctor. But I am just... I'm certain the mold and that wiring have poisoned everything I own. I can't stop it from getting worse because it's already in everything, the damage is done. And I can't stop Gary. I can just tell he and the rest of the neighbors are watching, just waiting for me to finally mess up for good. There's just nothing I can do about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any control over what happens to you and your house?\n\nPatient: Control? No, no control at all. I mean, how can I? Everything is already ruined. I haven't been able to keep up with any of it... the repairs, paying the bills, none of it. My wife, she’s had to take over everything. All the decisions I used to handle, she’s doing it now. She's been so stressed, I can see it in her face. She used to love gardening to relax, you know? She had these beautiful tomato plants out back, these huge Big Boys and some cherry tomatoes. We used to make our own salsa every August. We'd get the jalapeños from the farmer's market downtown... the one they hold on Saturdays next to the old courthouse. They have this guy there who sells the most incredible raw honey. He keeps his bees out by the county line, near where that old drive-in theater used to be before it burned down in the nineties. I couldn't let them in. Because if they came in, they'd see the poison, or they'd track more of it around, and Gary would see them, and... I just couldn't do it. So, no. I bought a pair of heavy thermal long johns once for a hunting trip, back when I used to hunt with my brother-in-law. Everything is ruined. And I can just tell the neighbors are watching, waiting to see when I mess up again. \n\nIt's like they have nothing better to do with their time. Phil drove this beat-up old El Camino, faded blue with a rusted-out tailgate. And they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting to see when I mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' whispering is directly about you or something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's about me. I mean, they're not whispering about the weather, you know? They look right at my house. It's like when you go to a restaurant and you know the waiter is talking about you to the bartender. We used to go to this diner, Mel's, over on 5th Street. They had the best cherry pie. Not that canned stuff, but real cherries, tart and sweet. My wife loved it. We'd go every Friday night, sit in the same red vinyl booth... booth number four, right by the jukebox. That jukebox had all the classics. I remember playing 'Dock of the Bay' on it once, and the needle got stuck, just kept repeating the same whistled note over and over until the cook had to come out and smack the side of the machine. He had this big grease stain on his apron...\n\nBut, uh, anyway, I know I've said this a hundred times today, and I know how crazy it sounds. I really do. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they know it. They're just watching, whispering, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control what you think about, or do your thoughts feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control my thoughts? I don't know... I guess I try to, but they just sort of go where they want. It's like trying to steer a car when the alignment is shot. I had this old Ford Taurus back in the late nineties, and the steering on that thing was just awful. If you took your hands off the wheel for even a second, it would pull hard to the right. I remember driving it down I-95 in the pouring rain, the wipers were making this terrible screeching noise because the rubber was completely worn down. I meant to replace them at AutoZone, but I kept forgetting. AutoZone always smelled like fresh tires and motor oil... I used to buy my windshield wiper fluid there, the kind that melts the ice in the winter. Winter used to be so much colder when I was a kid...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? My thoughts... right. I don't know if they control me, exactly. But I can't stop thinking about the house. I know I keep bringing it up, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. I can't stop thinking about it because it's true, and I know the neighbors are just waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, or do they seem disjointed and hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Connected? I mean, they make sense to me, mostly. It's just... there's a lot of them. Like a string of lights where one bulb goes out, and you have to check every single one to find the bad one. We had these old Christmas lights when I was a kid, the big glass ones, C9s I think they called them. They got so hot you could actually burn your fingers on them. My dad used to string them up around the gutters every Thanksgiving weekend. He had this aluminum ladder that was always wobbly on the left side because the rubber foot fell off. He tried fixing it with duct tape, but duct tape never really holds up in the cold, does it? It just gets brittle and peels right off. I used to hate holding the bottom of that ladder, my hands would just freeze... \n\nBut, uh, disjointed... I don't know. Maybe they are. It's hard to tell. But the main thing, the thing that never leaves my head—and I know I keep saying this, I know exactly how crazy it sounds—but I am absolutely positive that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated and ruined. And those neighbors, they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting for me to make another mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one thought?\n\nPatient: Focused? Yeah, I guess it's hard. It's like... it's like trying to watch television when the signal keeps dropping out. You know, back before cable, when you had to mess with the rabbit ears? My granddad had this old Zenith console TV. The thing was a piece of furniture, solid wood, weighed a ton. We used to have to wrap aluminum foil around the tips of the antennas just to get channel 3 to come in clear so we could watch the evening news. He always watched Walter Cronkite. Said he was the only man on television you could trust. He had this big recliner, olive green corduroy, and it squeaked every time he leaned back. I tried oiling the springs once with WD-40, but it just made the whole living room smell like a garage... \n\nBut, um, staying focused... I don't really know. I guess I lose track of things sometimes. But the one thing I can't lose track of—and I know you're probably sick of hearing it, I know how it sounds—but I am absolutely sure that the mold and the bad wiring in my house have poisoned every single thing I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they're just waiting, watching me, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you're worried about them, or because you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Worried about them? I mean, yeah, I'm worried, but it's more like... it's just always there. Like a hum in the background that you can't turn off. Reminds me of this old refrigerator I had in my first apartment. It was this ugly avocado green color, from the seventies, and the compressor was going bad. It made this constant, low buzzing sound, day and night. At first, it drove me absolutely up the wall, but eventually, I sort of got used to it. Until it finally died right before a long weekend, and all my groceries went bad. I had just bought a whole gallon of milk, too. Milk used to come in those thick glass bottles when I was really little, and the cream would rise to the top... I remember the milkman leaving them on the porch in this little wire carrier. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop thinking about it. Yeah, I can't stop. Because it's not just a worry, it's... it's a fact. I know I've said it a hundred times, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Every single thing. And the neighbors, I can tell they're just watching me, waiting to see when I mess up again. They're just waiting for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you can't stop, or because you believe it's really happening?\n\nPatient: Because it's really happening? Yeah. I mean, it's not just a feeling or a worry. It's like... it's like when you know something is wrong with your car, but the mechanic tells you it's fine. I had this '98 Ford Taurus back in the day, and the transmission kept slipping. The guy at the shop—Earl, I think his name was, he had this grease stain on his cap that looked exactly like the state of Nevada—he kept telling me it was just the cold weather making it shift hard. But you can feel it, you know? When the gears are just grinding together. I ended up breaking down on the interstate right near the county line. Had to wait three hours for a tow truck in the freezing rain. The driver was eating these little powdered donuts and got white sugar powder all over my steering wheel and the floor mats. I never could get that powder completely out of the carpet... \n\nBut, um... yeah. Because it's real. I know I keep repeating myself today, and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you. You probably think I've completely lost my mind. But I am absolutely certain that it's really happening. The mold, the wiring in that house—it actually poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors, they aren't just in my head either. I can tell they're out there watching me, just waiting to see when I'm gonna mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever actually seen the neighbors doing something that makes you think they're watching you, or is it just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: Seen them? Well, it's not just a feeling, I mean... it's like when you're looking through binoculars and you just know something's moving in the trees. I used to have this really nice pair of Bushnell binoculars. Heavy ones, with the black rubber coating on the outside. My uncle gave them to me right after I got my associate's degree. We used to go out to Miller's Pond in the early mornings to look for herons. Have you ever seen a great blue heron up close? They look exactly like dinosaurs. Just these long, scrawny legs and these sharp, spear-like beaks. They stand so perfectly still in the shallow water you'd think they were statues. Anyway, I dropped those binoculars on the concrete driveway a few years back and cracked the right lens right down the middle. I tried to fix it with a little dab of superglue, but it just made everything look foggy and gave me a massive headache whenever I tried to look through them...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, seeing the neighbors. I don't need binoculars for that. Look, I know I'm a broken record today. I know I keep saying the exact same thing, and I know how completely far-fetched it sounds to you. But I am absolutely certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors... I can just tell they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. I can just tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors would still be watching you if you moved to a different house?\n\nPatient: Moved to a different house? I... I don't know. Maybe? But moving is such a hassle. I helped my cousin move a few years ago. He had this massive, solid oak dining table. Must have weighed three hundred pounds. We had to pivot it around this tiny landing in his apartment building, and my cousin—he’s always been a little clumsy—he slipped on a piece of junk mail that was on the floor. It was one of those glossy circulars for a pizza place, the kind with the coupons you have to cut out. He went down hard and the table pinned his leg against the banister. We had to call the fire department to come get it off him. The paramedics gave him a little ice pack that you squeeze to make it cold... I always wondered what kind of chemicals are inside those things to make them freeze instantly like that. It can't be good for the environment.\n\nBut, uh, moving away... I mean, I know I've said this a dozen times already, and I know how completely crazy it sounds. You probably think I'm making it all up. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors... even if I moved, I feel like they'd still be watching me. They're just waiting for me to mess up again. I can just feel it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Actually, it was the exact same day I bought this heavy-duty bleach to try and scrub the walls. People think bleach kills mold, but it really doesn't. He slipped on some black ice outside a gas station and broke two vertebrae. He had to wear one of those awful halo braces for months. It looked so uncomfortable, having metal literally screwed into your skull... I don't know how he slept. He said he had to sleep sitting straight up in a brown leather recliner for half a year. \n\nBut... wait, what were we talking about? Oh, the house. Right. Like I said before, I know it sounds far-fetched. I know exactly how it sounds to a doctor. But I am just... I'm certain the mold and that wiring have poisoned everything I own. I can't stop it from getting worse because it's already in everything, the damage is done. And I can't stop Gary. I can just tell he and the rest of the neighbors are watching, just waiting for me to finally mess up for good. There's just nothing I can do about any of it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have any control over what happens to you and your house?\n\nPatient: Control? No, no control at all. I mean, how can I? Everything is already ruined. I haven't been able to keep up with any of it... the repairs, paying the bills, none of it. My wife, she’s had to take over everything. All the decisions I used to handle, she’s doing it now. She's been so stressed, I can see it in her face. She used to love gardening to relax, you know? She had these beautiful tomato plants out back, these huge Big Boys and some cherry tomatoes. We used to make our own salsa every August. We'd get the jalapeños from the farmer's market downtown... the one they hold on Saturdays next to the old courthouse. They have this guy there who sells the most incredible raw honey. He keeps his bees out by the county line, near where that old drive-in theater used to be before it burned down in the nineties. I remember going there when I was a kid to see a double feature, sitting in the back of my dad's station wagon... \n\nBut, right, the house. Yeah, I can't do anything about it. Things got so bad that my wife actually called one of those crisis teams on me. She had to. There was some kind of safety inspection they wanted to do at the house, and I just couldn't cooperate. I couldn't let them in. Because if they came in, they'd see the poison, or they'd track more of it around, and Gary would see them, and... I just couldn't do it. So, no. I don't have any control left.\n\nDoctor: Do you think Gary or the neighbors are plotting against you specifically?\n\nPatient: Plotting? I don't know if they have, like, secret meetings about it, but yes, it's me they're focused on. Like I keep saying... and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you, I really do... but I am absolutely certain they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. \n\nGary especially. He'll stand out by his mailbox forever, just staring at my place. He gets his mail from that same guy we've had on our route for fifteen years. Carl, I think his name is. Carl always wears those blue uniform shorts, even in the dead of December. I don't understand how his legs don't freeze. I bought a pair of heavy thermal long johns once for a hunting trip, back when I used to hunt with my brother-in-law. We went up to this little cabin near the Canadian border, and it was so cold the pipes froze solid the very first night. We had to scoop snow into a pot and melt it on the woodstove just to make our morning coffee. The coffee tasted awful, like pine needles and ash, but we drank it anyway because we were just shivering so hard... \n\nWait, why was I talking about the cabin? Oh, Gary. Right. No, he's watching me. They all are. And they know my house is ruined. I know I've said it a hundred times today, but the mold and that bad wiring... it has poisoned everything I own. Everything is just contaminated.\n\nDoctor: Do you think Gary or the neighbors want to harm you?\n\nPatient: Harm me? Like... physically? No, I don't think they're going to come over here and, you know, attack me or anything. It's not like that. It's more like they're just waiting for me to fail. Like I told you, I know how crazy it sounds, I really do. But I'm telling you, I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Everything is ruined. And I can just tell the neighbors are watching, waiting to see when I mess up again. \n\nIt's like they have nothing better to do with their time. Especially since the neighborhood association stopped doing those monthly block parties. They used to have them every first Sunday of the month, right down at the end of the cul-de-sac. Someone would always bring this massive inflatable bounce house for the kids. One time, it got really windy, and the stakes came loose, and the whole thing almost blew into Mrs. Gable's prized rose bushes. She would have been furious. She spends hours out there with these tiny little pruning shears, clipping away at them. She won first prize at the county fair three years in a row for her yellow roses. My wife tried growing roses once, but the Japanese beetles just decimated them. We tried spraying them with this organic neem oil stuff, but it smelled terrible, like rancid garlic and peanut butter...\n\nBut, yeah, no, they don't want to hurt me physically. They're just watching. Waiting for me to finally crack.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors are talking about you specifically when they're outside?\n\nPatient: Talking about me? Yeah... I mean, I see them out there, leaning over the property lines. They whisper. It’s like high school all over again. My daughter hated high school for that exact reason. She went to West Monroe, and the girls there were just brutal. She had this one friend, Sarah, who spread a rumor about her stealing a graphing calculator from the math lab. Can you believe that? A TI-84. Those things were like a hundred bucks back then. I remember buying it at Circuit City before they all closed down. Circuit City had that big red plug logo... I used to love wandering the aisles there on weekends, looking at the huge stereo systems. We bought our first CD player there in the nineties, it was a Sony, held five discs at once... \n\nBut, um, what were we... right, the neighbors. Yes, they talk about me. I know I keep saying this today, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but they are definitely watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. They know about the house. They know that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. They're just standing out there whispering about it, waiting for me to finally lose it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' conversations are specifically about you when they whisper?\n\nPatient: Yeah, when they whisper... I mean, what else would they be whispering about? It's always when I'm out there or when they think I'm looking out the window. They huddle up. Just like those guys at the hardware store used to do. I worked at a True Value for about six months back in my twenties. The manager, this guy named Phil, he was always whispering with the cashiers. Phil drove this beat-up old El Camino, faded blue with a rusted-out tailgate. He was so proud of that car, always talking about how he was going to restore it, put a new engine in it, paint it cherry red. He kept a bucket of those pine tree air fresheners in the cab, so it always smelled like cheap cologne and cedar... \n\nAnyway, I don't even know why I thought of Phil. But yes, the neighbors. They're whispering about me. Like I keep telling you—and I know it sounds completely far-fetched, I really do—but I am certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting to see when I mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors' whispering is directly about you or something else?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it's about me. I mean, they're not whispering about the weather, you know? They look right at my house. It's like when you go to a restaurant and you know the waiter is talking about you to the bartender. We used to go to this diner, Mel's, over on 5th Street. They had the best cherry pie. Not that canned stuff, but real cherries, tart and sweet. My wife loved it. We'd go every Friday night, sit in the same red vinyl booth... booth number four, right by the jukebox. That jukebox had all the classics. I remember playing 'Dock of the Bay' on it once, and the needle got stuck, just kept repeating the same whistled note over and over until the cook had to come out and smack the side of the machine. He had this big grease stain on his apron...\n\nBut, uh, anyway, I know I've said this a hundred times today, and I know how crazy it sounds. I really do. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they know it. They're just watching, whispering, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control what you think about, or do your thoughts feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control my thoughts? I don't know... I guess I try to, but they just sort of go where they want. It's like trying to steer a car when the alignment is shot. I had this old Ford Taurus back in the late nineties, and the steering on that thing was just awful. If you took your hands off the wheel for even a second, it would pull hard to the right. I remember driving it down I-95 in the pouring rain, the wipers were making this terrible screeching noise because the rubber was completely worn down. I meant to replace them at AutoZone, but I kept forgetting. AutoZone always smelled like fresh tires and motor oil... I used to buy my windshield wiper fluid there, the kind that melts the ice in the winter. Winter used to be so much colder when I was a kid...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? My thoughts... right. I don't know if they control me, exactly. But I can't stop thinking about the house. I know I keep bringing it up, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. I can't stop thinking about it because it's true, and I know the neighbors are just waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, or do they seem disjointed and hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Connected? I mean, they make sense to me, mostly. It's just... there's a lot of them. Like a string of lights where one bulb goes out, and you have to check every single one to find the bad one. We had these old Christmas lights when I was a kid, the big glass ones, C9s I think they called them. They got so hot you could actually burn your fingers on them. My dad used to string them up around the gutters every Thanksgiving weekend. He had this aluminum ladder that was always wobbly on the left side because the rubber foot fell off. He tried fixing it with duct tape, but duct tape never really holds up in the cold, does it? It just gets brittle and peels right off. I used to hate holding the bottom of that ladder, my hands would just freeze... \n\nBut, uh, disjointed... I don't know. Maybe they are. It's hard to tell. But the main thing, the thing that never leaves my head—and I know I keep saying this, I know exactly how crazy it sounds—but I am absolutely positive that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated and ruined. And those neighbors, they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting for me to make another mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one thought?\n\nPatient: Focused? Yeah, I guess it's hard. It's like... it's like trying to watch television when the signal keeps dropping out. You know, back before cable, when you had to mess with the rabbit ears? My granddad had this old Zenith console TV. The thing was a piece of furniture, solid wood, weighed a ton. We used to have to wrap aluminum foil around the tips of the antennas just to get channel 3 to come in clear so we could watch the evening news. He always watched Walter Cronkite. Said he was the only man on television you could trust. He had this big recliner, olive green corduroy, and it squeaked every time he leaned back. I tried oiling the springs once with WD-40, but it just made the whole living room smell like a garage... \n\nBut, um, staying focused... I don't really know. I guess I lose track of things sometimes. But the one thing I can't lose track of—and I know you're probably sick of hearing it, I know how it sounds—but I am absolutely sure that the mold and the bad wiring in my house have poisoned every single thing I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they're just waiting, watching me, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you're worried about them, or because you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Worried about them? I mean, yeah, I'm worried, but it's more like... it's just always there. Like a hum in the background that you can't turn off. Reminds me of this old refrigerator I had in my first apartment. It was this ugly avocado green color, from the seventies, and the compressor was going bad. It made this constant, low buzzing sound, day and night. At first, it drove me absolutely up the wall, but eventually, I sort of got used to it. Until it finally died right before a long weekend, and all my groceries went bad. I had just bought a whole gallon of milk, too. Milk used to come in those thick glass bottles when I was really little, and the cream would rise to the top... I remember the milkman leaving them on the porch in this little wire carrier. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop thinking about it. Yeah, I can't stop. Because it's not just a worry, it's... it's a fact. I know I've said it a hundred times, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Every single thing. And the neighbors, I can tell they're just watching me, waiting to see when I mess up again. They're just waiting for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you can't stop, or because you believe it's really happening?\n\nPatient: Because it's really happening? Yeah. I mean, it's not just a feeling or a worry. It's like... it's like when you know something is wrong with your car, but the mechanic tells you it's fine. I had this '98 Ford Taurus back in the day, and the transmission kept slipping. The guy at the shop—Earl, I think his name was, he had this grease stain on his cap that looked exactly like the state of Nevada—he kept telling me it was just the cold weather making it shift hard. But you can feel it, you know? When the gears are just grinding together. I ended up breaking down on the interstate right near the county line. Had to wait three hours for a tow truck in the freezing rain. The driver was eating these little powdered donuts and got white sugar powder all over my steering wheel and the floor mats. I never could get that powder completely out of the carpet... \n\nBut, um... yeah. Because it's real. I know I keep repeating myself today, and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you. You probably think I've completely lost my mind. But I am absolutely certain that it's really happening. The mold, the wiring in that house—it actually poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors, they aren't just in my head either. I can tell they're out there watching me, just waiting to see when I'm gonna mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever actually seen the neighbors doing something that makes you think they're watching you, or is it just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: Seen them? Well, it's not just a feeling, I mean... it's like when you're looking through binoculars and you just know something's moving in the trees. I used to have this really nice pair of Bushnell binoculars. Heavy ones, with the black rubber coating on the outside. My uncle gave them to me right after I got my associate's degree. We used to go out to Miller's Pond in the early mornings to look for herons. Have you ever seen a great blue heron up close? They look exactly like dinosaurs. Just these long, scrawny legs and these sharp, spear-like beaks. They stand so perfectly still in the shallow water you'd think they were statues. Anyway, I dropped those binoculars on the concrete driveway a few years back and cracked the right lens right down the middle. I tried to fix it with a little dab of superglue, but it just made everything look foggy and gave me a massive headache whenever I tried to look through them...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about? Oh, seeing the neighbors. I don't need binoculars for that. Look, I know I'm a broken record today. I know I keep saying the exact same thing, and I know how completely far-fetched it sounds to you. But I am absolutely certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors... I can just tell they are watching me, just waiting to see when I mess up again. I can just tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the neighbors would still be watching you if you moved to a different house?\n\nPatient: Moved to a different house? I... I don't know. Maybe? But moving is such a hassle. I helped my cousin move a few years ago. He had this massive, solid oak dining table. Must have weighed three hundred pounds. We had to pivot it around this tiny landing in his apartment building, and my cousin—he’s always been a little clumsy—he slipped on a piece of junk mail that was on the floor. It was one of those glossy circulars for a pizza place, the kind with the coupons you have to cut out. He went down hard and the table pinned his leg against the banister. We had to call the fire department to come get it off him. The paramedics gave him a little ice pack that you squeeze to make it cold... I always wondered what kind of chemicals are inside those things to make them freeze instantly like that. It can't be good for the environment.\n\nBut, uh, moving away... I mean, I know I've said this a dozen times already, and I know how completely crazy it sounds. You probably think I'm making it all up. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors... even if I moved, I feel like they'd still be watching me. They're just waiting for me to mess up again. I can just feel it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. A possibility? I... no. I mean, I tried to see if I could salvage some things. I bought a whole pack of those yellow sponges, the ones with the green scrubby side on the back. Other people? Well, my wife doesn't even believe me, so I highly doubt anyone else does. I tried explaining it to my brother-in-law, Dave, last Thanksgiving. We were standing out on the deck while he deep-fried a turkey. Have you ever had deep-fried turkey? It's incredibly juicy, but it's so dangerous to make. You have to measure the oil displacement with water first, otherwise when you drop the bird in, boiling peanut oil spills everywhere and catches fire. Dave actually singed off his eyebrows doing it a few years ago. He looked so surprised for about three months until they grew back. He works as an actuary, so you'd think he'd be better at assessing risk, but I guess it doesn't translate to poultry. He brought over this weird cranberry sauce that had jalapeños in it, too. I don't know why people can't just leave traditional recipes alone...

Anyway, what was the question? Oh, other people believing me. No, nobody does. And look, I know I'm beating a dead horse here. I know I keep saying this over and over, and I know how completely far-fetched it sounds. But I am absolutely certain the mold and the wiring in that house have poisoned everything I own. It's in everything. And those neighbors... they don't believe me either, but I can tell they're watching me. They're just sitting over there, waiting to see when I mess up again.

We'd go every Friday night, sit in the same red vinyl booth... booth number four, right by the jukebox. That jukebox had all the classics. But I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they know it. They're just watching, whispering, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control what you think about, or do your thoughts feel like they're controlling you?\n\nPatient: Control my thoughts? I don't know... I guess I try to, but they just sort of go where they want. It's like trying to steer a car when the alignment is shot. I had this old Ford Taurus back in the late nineties, and the steering on that thing was just awful. If you took your hands off the wheel for even a second, it would pull hard to the right. I remember driving it down I-95 in the pouring rain, the wipers were making this terrible screeching noise because the rubber was completely worn down. I meant to replace them at AutoZone, but I kept forgetting. AutoZone always smelled like fresh tires and motor oil... I used to buy my windshield wiper fluid there, the kind that melts the ice in the winter. Winter used to be so much colder when I was a kid...\n\nBut, um, what was the question? My thoughts... right. I don't know if they control me, exactly. But I can't stop thinking about the house. I know I keep bringing it up, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the bad wiring have completely poisoned everything I own. I can't stop thinking about it because it's true, and I know the neighbors are just waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are connected and make sense to you, or do they seem disjointed and hard to follow?\n\nPatient: Connected? I mean, they make sense to me, mostly. It's just... there's a lot of them. Like a string of lights where one bulb goes out, and you have to check every single one to find the bad one. We had these old Christmas lights when I was a kid, the big glass ones, C9s I think they called them. They got so hot you could actually burn your fingers on them. My dad used to string them up around the gutters every Thanksgiving weekend. He had this aluminum ladder that was always wobbly on the left side because the rubber foot fell off. He tried fixing it with duct tape, but duct tape never really holds up in the cold, does it? It just gets brittle and peels right off. I used to hate holding the bottom of that ladder, my hands would just freeze... \n\nBut, uh, disjointed... I don't know. Maybe they are. It's hard to tell. But the main thing, the thing that never leaves my head—and I know I keep saying this, I know exactly how crazy it sounds—but I am absolutely positive that the mold and the bad wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated and ruined. And those neighbors, they know it. They're just watching and whispering, waiting for me to make another mistake.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stay focused on one thought?\n\nPatient: Focused? Yeah, I guess it's hard. It's like... it's like trying to watch television when the signal keeps dropping out. You know, back before cable, when you had to mess with the rabbit ears? My granddad had this old Zenith console TV. The thing was a piece of furniture, solid wood, weighed a ton. We used to have to wrap aluminum foil around the tips of the antennas just to get channel 3 to come in clear so we could watch the evening news. He always watched Walter Cronkite. Said he was the only man on television you could trust. He had this big recliner, olive green corduroy, and it squeaked every time he leaned back. I tried oiling the springs once with WD-40, but it just made the whole living room smell like a garage... \n\nBut, um, staying focused... I don't really know. I guess I lose track of things sometimes. But the one thing I can't lose track of—and I know you're probably sick of hearing it, I know how it sounds—but I am absolutely sure that the mold and the bad wiring in my house have poisoned every single thing I own. It's all ruined. And the neighbors, they're just waiting, watching me, waiting for me to mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you're worried about them, or because you can't stop thinking about them?\n\nPatient: Worried about them? I mean, yeah, I'm worried, but it's more like... it's just always there. Like a hum in the background that you can't turn off. Reminds me of this old refrigerator I had in my first apartment. It was this ugly avocado green color, from the seventies, and the compressor was going bad. It made this constant, low buzzing sound, day and night. At first, it drove me absolutely up the wall, but eventually, I sort of got used to it. Until it finally died right before a long weekend, and all my groceries went bad. I had just bought a whole gallon of milk, too. Milk used to come in those thick glass bottles when I was really little, and the cream would rise to the top... I remember the milkman leaving them on the porch in this little wire carrier. \n\nBut, uh, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop thinking about it. Yeah, I can't stop. Because it's not just a worry, it's... it's a fact. I know I've said it a hundred times, and I know you probably think it's completely far-fetched, but I am absolutely certain that the mold and the wiring in that house have completely poisoned everything I own. Every single thing. And the neighbors, I can tell they're just watching me, waiting to see when I mess up again. They're just waiting for it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're thinking about the mold and wiring because you can't stop, or because you believe it's really happening?\n\nPatient: Because it's really happening? Yeah. I mean, it's not just a feeling or a worry. It's like... it's like when you know something is wrong with your car, but the mechanic tells you it's fine. I had this '98 Ford Taurus back in the day, and the transmission kept slipping. The guy at the shop—Earl, I think his name was, he had this grease stain on his cap that looked exactly like the state of Nevada—he kept telling me it was just the cold weather making it shift hard. But you can feel it, you know? When the gears are just grinding together. I ended up breaking down on the interstate right near the county line. Had to wait three hours for a tow truck in the freezing rain. The driver was eating these little powdered donuts and got white sugar powder all over my steering wheel and the floor mats. I never could get that powder completely out of the carpet... \n\nBut, um... yeah. Because it's real. I know I keep repeating myself today, and I know exactly how far-fetched it sounds to you. You probably think I've completely lost my mind. But I am absolutely certain that it's really happening. The mold, the wiring in that house—it actually poisoned everything I own. It's all contaminated. And the neighbors, they aren't just in my head either. I can tell they're out there watching me, just waiting to see when I'm gonna mess up again.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever actually seen the neighbors doing something that makes you think they're watching you, or is it just a feeling you have?\n\nPatient: Seen them? Well, it's not just a feeling, I mean... it's like when you're looking through binoculars and you just know something's moving in the trees. I used to have this really nice pair of Bushnell binoculars. Heavy ones, with the black rubber coating on the outside. My uncle gave them to me right after I got my associate's degree. We used to go out to Miller's Pond in the early mornings to look for herons. Have you ever seen a great blue heron up close? They look exactly like dinosaurs. Just these long, scrawny legs and these sharp, spear-like beaks. They stand so perfectly still in the shallow water you'd think they were statues. Anyway, I dropped those binoculars on the concrete driveway a few years back and cracked the right lens right down the middle. I tried to fix it with a little dab of superglue, but it just made everything look foggy and gave me a massive headache whenever I tried to look through them...\n\nBut, uh, what were we talking about?
